Multi-bubble solutions for the Dirichlet problem of the H \displaystyle H -system with higher degree
Xiang Fang, Juncheng Wei, Youquan Zheng, Yifu Zhou

Abstract
. We consider a Dirichlet problem of the H \displaystyle H -system { Δ ​ v = 2 ​ v x ∧ v y in ​ 𝒟 , v = ε ​ g ~ on ​ ∂ 𝒟 , \begin{cases}\Delta v=2v_{x}\wedge v_{y}~&\text{ in }\mathcal{D},\\ v=\varepsilon\tilde{g}~&\text{ on }\partial{\mathcal{D}},\end{cases} where 𝒟 ⊂ ℝ 2 \displaystyle\mathcal{D}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2} is the unit disk, v : 𝒟 → ℝ 3 \displaystyle v:\mathcal{D}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3} , and g ~ : ∂ 𝒟 → ℝ 3 \displaystyle\tilde{g}:{\partial}\mathcal{D}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3} is a given smooth map. As ε → 0 + \displaystyle\varepsilon\to 0^{+} , we construct multi-bubble solutions concentrating at distinct points, taking around each point the profile of degree 2 H \displaystyle H -bubble. This gives a partial answer to a conjecture due to Brezis-Coron [ 4 ] and Chanillo-Malchiodi [ 14 ] concerning the limiting configuration in the case of higher degrees. This seems to be the first construction in employing higher-degree harmonic maps as the primary configurations.



1.
Introduction

Let
\displaystyle\Omega
be a smooth bounded domain in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and maps
\displaystyle v:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\displaystyle\tilde{g}:\partial{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\displaystyle\tilde{g}
being smooth. We consider the Dirichlet boundary problem

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\Delta v=2H(v)v_{x}\wedge v_{y}~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
v=\tilde{g}~&\text{ on }\partial{\Omega},\end{cases}

where
\displaystyle H(v)
is a given scalar function,
\displaystyle v_{x}
,
\displaystyle v_{y}
denote the partial derivatives of
\displaystyle v
, and
\displaystyle\wedge
denotes the cross-product in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The system (
1.1
) arises from a classical geometric variational problem of finding parametric surfaces in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with prescribed mean curvature
\displaystyle H(v)
in the conformal representation. The existence and optimal estimates for the Dirichlet problem have been intensively investigated in many seminal works, such as Heinz
[
23
]
, Hildebrandt
[
25
,
26
]
, Gulliver-Spruck
[
21
,
22
]
, Steffen
[
41
,
40
]
and Wente
[
48
]
. See also Struwe
[
44
]
and Duzaar-Steffen
[
17
]
for existence results of the associated Plateau problem.

Via Pohozaev-type identity, it was proved by Wente
[
49
]
and Chanillo-Malchiodi
[
14
]
that there exists no non-trivial solution on simply connected domain when
\displaystyle\tilde{g}=0
. When
\displaystyle H(v)=1
and
\displaystyle\|\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}<1
, Hildebrandt constructed a solution with minimal energy in
[
26
]
, while Brezis-Coron
[
5
]
, Steffen
[
42
]
, and Struwe
[
45
]
proved the existence of large energy solutions. A non-existence result was shown by Heinz
[
24
]
for
\displaystyle\|\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}>1
, and thus the condition
\displaystyle\|\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}<1
is sharp.

For general
\displaystyle H(v)
, the existence of solutions was studied in a series of papers by Caldiroli-Musina
[
7
,
6
,
8
,
33
,
12
]
, and their method is based on the variational perturbative method, which was introduced by Ambrosetti and Badiale in
[
1
]
. Concentration phenomenon, such as bubbling and multi-bubble solutions were constructed in Caldiroli-Musina

[
9
]
and Chanillo-Malchiodi
[
14
]
. Under assumptions on general
\displaystyle H(v)
, interior and boundary regularity were investigated by Bethuel
[
3
]
, Strzelecki
[
47
]
, Musina
[
34
]
, Müller-Schikorra
[
32
]
and their references. Furthermore, the asymptotic behavior of the solutions to (
1.1
) was studied, for example, in Sasahara
[
36
]
, Isobe
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
, and Caldiroli-Musina
[
10
,
11
]
and the references therein, where some important quantities were computed and found. In
[
35
]
, Rivière found a remarkable structure for two-dimensional conformally invariant nonlinear elliptic PDEs, including harmonic map equation and the prescribed mean curvature equation. Various regularity results were proved based on this structure; see Schikorra-Strzelecki
[
37
]
for the regularity theory for higher dimensional
\displaystyle H
-systems as well as
\displaystyle n
-harmonic maps into compact Riemannian manifolds. Geometric objects such as the CMC surfaces in related settings have been investigated, for instance, in
[
18
,
30
]
. We refer the interested readers to the comprehensive monographs of Struwe
[
45
,
46
]
, Duzaar-Steffen
[
16
]
, Steffen
[
43
]
, Bethuel-Caldiroli-Guida
[
2
]
and the references therein for more geometrical motivations and general formulations.

In the seminal work
[
4
]
, Brezis and Coron
considered (
1.1
) with
\displaystyle H\equiv 1
and
\displaystyle\Omega=\mathcal{D}
being the unit disk and proved that the sequence
\displaystyle u_{n}
, solving (
1.1
) with vanishing boundary data
\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{n}
in
\displaystyle H^{1/2}(\partial\mathcal{D})\cap L^{\infty}(\partial\mathcal{D})
, converges to a finite and connected union of unit spheres. No further precise information about the limiting configuration was obtained, so they asked whether every configuration of spheres can be obtained as a limit of solutions
\displaystyle u_{n}
for a suitable sequence of vanishing boundary data
\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{n}
. This question was later answered partially by Chanillo and Malchiodi
[
14
]
in the case of degree
\displaystyle 1
, where they investigated the singularly perturbed problem

(1.2)

\begin{cases}\Delta v=2v_{x}\wedge v_{y}~&\text{ in }\mathcal{D},\\
v=\varepsilon\tilde{g}~&\text{ on }\partial{\mathcal{D}},\end{cases}

as
\displaystyle\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. Above problem can be formulated variationally as follows. Let
\displaystyle g
be the harmonic extension of
\displaystyle\tilde{g}
, i.e.

\begin{cases}\Delta g=0~&\text{ in }\mathcal{D},\\
g=\tilde{g}~&\text{ on }\partial{\mathcal{D}}.\end{cases}

Let
\displaystyle v=u+\varepsilon g
be a solution of (
1.2
), then
\displaystyle u
solves

\begin{cases}\Delta u=2(u_{x}+\varepsilon g_{x})\wedge(u_{y}+\varepsilon g_{y})~&\text{ in }\mathcal{D},\\
u=0~&\text{ on }\partial{\mathcal{D}}.\end{cases}

Its associated Euler functional
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}:H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{3})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is given by

(1.3)

I_{\varepsilon}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{D}}|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\int_{\mathcal{D}}u\cdot(u_{x}\wedge u_{y})+\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}}u\cdot(u_{x}\wedge g_{y}+g_{x}\wedge u_{y})+2\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\mathcal{D}}u\cdot(g_{x}\wedge g_{y}).

By analyzing
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}(u)
, Chanillo and Malchiodi
[
14
]
established an asymptotic Morse theory and constructed solutions in the limit with multi-bubble configuration. More precisely, given any configuration of unit spheres,
all
passing through the origin in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, there exists a sequence of boundary data
\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{n}
such that the solutions
\displaystyle u_{n}
, with degree
\displaystyle 1
bubbling profile, converge to the configuration in the Hausdorff sense, and their construction thus gives a partial answer to the question raised by Brezis-Coron.

A natural question is the possible scenario in the case of higher degree. In this paper, we are interested in the bubbling phenomena of (
1.2
) as
\displaystyle\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
in the degree 2 case. Our aim is to construct bubbling solutions with multiple degree
\displaystyle 2
profiles concentrating at different points in
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
. Recall that the fundamental solution, known as
\displaystyle H
-bubble, of the equation

(1.4)

\Delta u=2u_{x}\wedge u_{y}~\text{ in }~\mathbb{R}^{2}

is the stereographic projection
\displaystyle\pi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}

\pi(z)=\pi(x,y)=\frac{1}{1+x^{2}+y^{2}}(2x,~2y,~x^{2}+y^{2}-1),\qquad z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Indeed, for the
\displaystyle H
-system in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with constant mean curvature
\displaystyle H=1
, finite-energy solutions were classified in the classical paper
[
4
]
by Brezis-Coron as

u(z)=\pi\left(\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}\right)+C,\quad z=(x,y)\equiv x+iy,

where
\displaystyle P
,
\displaystyle Q
are polynomials of
\displaystyle z
, and
\displaystyle C
is a constant vector in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
From this result, we know that a typical class of solutions to (
1.4
)
are
\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{(m)}(z)=\pi\left(z^{m}\right)
for
\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\displaystyle m
is the degree/homotopy class of the map, defined as

{\rm deg}(u)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}u\cdot(u_{y}\wedge u_{x}).

The degree 2
\displaystyle H
-bubble
\displaystyle\mathcal{W}:=\pi(z^{2}):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
can be then written as

(1.5)

z=(x,y)\to\left(\frac{2(x-y)(x+y)}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1},\frac{4xy}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1},\frac{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}-1}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1}\right).

The degree 2 bubble defined in (
1.5
) of equation (
1.4
) has more invariance under rigid motions than the degree
\displaystyle 1
case. In fact, it is invariant under the following transformations. If
\displaystyle u(z)
is a degree 2 solution of (
1.4
) and identifying
\displaystyle z
as a complex number, then all of these maps

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mbox{ (1) }u\left(\frac{z}{\mu}\right)~\mbox{ for all }~\mu>0,\quad\mbox{ (2) }u(z+\xi)~\mbox{ for }~\xi\in\mathbb{C},\quad\mbox{ (3) }u\left(\frac{z-a|z|^{2}}{1-2a\cdot z+|a|^{2}|z|^{2}}\right)~\mbox{ for }~a\in\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mbox{ (4) }u(z^{2}+p)~\mbox{ for }~p\in\mathbb{C},\quad\mbox{ (5) }\mathcal{Q}u~\mbox{ for a rotation}~\mathcal{Q}\in SO(3),\quad\mbox{ (6) }u+C~\mbox{ for any constant vector}~C

are still degree 2 solutions. That is to say, the function

(1.6)

\mathcal{Q}\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}(z)+C=\mathcal{Q}\pi\left(\frac{\left(\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}-a\left|\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}}{\left(1-2a\cdot\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}+|a|^{2}\left|\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}}+p\right)+C

is a
\displaystyle H
-bubble with degree 2, forming
\displaystyle 13
-parameter family of solutions. Here,
\displaystyle z=(x,y)
,
\displaystyle\mu>0
,
\displaystyle\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
,
\displaystyle a=(a_{1},a_{2})
,
\displaystyle p=(p_{1},p_{2})
. The functions
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}
are mountain-pass critical points of the functional

(1.7)

\bar{I}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\cdot(u_{x}\wedge u_{y}),\quad u\in\mathcal{X},

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
denotes the functional space

\mathcal{X}=\left\{u\in L_{{\rm loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{3}):\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|u|^{2}}{(1+|z|^{2})^{2}}<+\infty\right\}.

The space
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
coincides with
\displaystyle H^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{3})
after inverse stereographic projection. When
\displaystyle a=0
,
\displaystyle p=0
, the function
\displaystyle\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}
has the explicit form

(1.8)

\delta_{\mu,\xi,0,0}(z)=\pi\left(\left(\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right)^{2}\right)=\left(\frac{2\left[\left(\frac{x-\xi_{1}}{\mu}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{y-\xi_{2}}{\mu}\right)^{2}\right]}{\left(\left|\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}+1},\frac{4\frac{(x-\xi_{1})(y-\xi_{2})}{\mu^{2}}}{\left(\left|\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}+1},\frac{\left(\left|\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}-1}{\left(\left|\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}+1}\right).

In the following, we simply denote
\displaystyle\delta_{\mu,\xi,0,0}
as
\displaystyle\delta_{\mu,\xi}
.
We now consider the linearized operator around the
\displaystyle H
-bubble with degree
\displaystyle 2
. The rotation matrix can be represented by
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}
as

\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\alpha&-\sin\alpha&0\\
\sin\alpha&\cos\alpha&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\cos\beta&-\sin\beta\\
0&\sin\beta&\cos\beta\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\gamma&0&\sin\gamma\\
0&1&0\\
-\sin\gamma&0&\cos\gamma\end{pmatrix},

where
\displaystyle\alpha
,
\displaystyle\beta
,
\displaystyle\gamma
are the angle of rotation around the coordinate
\displaystyle z
-axis,
\displaystyle x
-axis,
\displaystyle y
-axis in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, respectively, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}\in SO(3)
.
Then modulo constant vectors in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
,

(1.9)

\displaystyle U_{A}(x,y)=\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}\pi\left(\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\left(\frac{x-\xi_{1}}{\mu}-a_{1}\left(\frac{(x-\xi_{1})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{(y-\xi_{2})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{y-\xi_{2}}{\mu}-a_{2}\left(\frac{(x-\xi_{1})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{(y-\xi_{2})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right)^{2}}{\left(1-2\left(\frac{a_{1}(x-\xi_{1})}{\mu}+\frac{a_{2}(y-\xi_{2})}{\mu}\right)+\left(a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}\right)\left(\frac{(x-\xi_{1})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{(y-\xi_{2})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{2\left(\frac{x-\xi_{1}}{\mu}-a_{1}\left(\frac{(x-\xi_{1})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{(y-\xi_{2})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right)\left(\frac{y-\xi_{2}}{\mu}-a_{2}\left(\frac{(x-\xi_{1})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{(y-\xi_{2})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right)}{\left(1-2\left(\frac{a_{1}(x-\xi_{1})}{\mu}+\frac{a_{2}(y-\xi_{2})}{\mu}\right)+\left(a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}\right)\left(\frac{(x-\xi_{1})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{(y-\xi_{2})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right)^{2}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\\
p_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)

gives a ten-parameter family of solutions with degree
\displaystyle 2
to the
\displaystyle H
-system (
1.4
) in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Here we denote the tuple of real numbers by

A:=(\mu,~\alpha,~\xi_{1},~\xi_{2},~p_{1},~p_{2},~a_{1},~a_{2},~\beta,~\gamma).

The first variations with respect to the parameters
\displaystyle\mu
,
\displaystyle\alpha
,
\displaystyle\xi_{1}
,
\displaystyle\xi_{2}
,
\displaystyle p_{1}
,
\displaystyle p_{2}
,
\displaystyle a_{1}
,
\displaystyle a_{2}
,
\displaystyle\beta
,
\displaystyle\gamma
are given respectively by

\displaystyle\displaystyle Z_{0,1}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\frac{4(x-y)(x+y)\left(x^{2}+y^{2}-1\right)\left(x^{2}+y^{2}+1\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{8xy\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}-1\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
-\frac{8\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},\qquad Z_{0,2}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{4xy}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1}\\
\frac{2(x-y)(x+y)}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1}\\
0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\displaystyle Z_{1,1}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\frac{4x\left(x^{4}-2x^{2}y^{2}-3y^{4}-1\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
-\frac{4y\left(-3x^{4}-2x^{2}y^{2}+y^{4}+1\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
-\frac{8x\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},\quad\qquad\quad Z_{1,2}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{4y\left(3x^{4}+2x^{2}y^{2}-y^{4}+1\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
-\frac{4x\left(x^{4}-2x^{2}y^{2}-3y^{4}+1\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
-\frac{8y\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\displaystyle Z_{2,1}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\frac{-2(x^{4}-6x^{2}y^{2}+y^{4}-1)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{-8xy(x-y)(x+y)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{4(x-y)(x+y)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},\quad\qquad\qquad Z_{2,2}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{-8xy(x-y)(x+y)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{2(x^{4}-6x^{2}y^{2}+y^{4}+1)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{8xy}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\displaystyle Z_{-1,1}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\frac{-4x\left(3\left(x^{4}+1\right)y^{2}+3x^{2}y^{4}+x^{2}\left(x^{4}-1\right)+y^{6}\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{-4y\left(x^{6}+3x^{4}y^{2}+3x^{2}\left(y^{4}-1\right)+y^{6}+y^{2}\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{8x\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\displaystyle Z_{-1,2}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\frac{4y\left(x^{6}+3x^{4}y^{2}+3x^{2}\left(y^{4}+1\right)+y^{6}-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{-4x\left(x^{6}+3x^{4}y^{2}+3x^{2}y^{4}+x^{2}+y^{6}-3y^{2}\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{8y\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}}{\left(\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},

(1.10)

\displaystyle\displaystyle Z_{-2,1}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\frac{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}-1}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1}\\
\frac{4xy}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad Z_{-2,2}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}-1}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1}\\
0\\
\frac{2(x-y)(x+y)}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}+1}\end{pmatrix}.

In other words,
\displaystyle Z_{k,j}
,
\displaystyle k=0,\pm 1,\pm 2
,
\displaystyle j=1,2
satisfy the linearized equation of (
1.4
) around
\displaystyle\mathcal{W}
defined by

(1.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle L_{\mathcal{W}}[v]:=\Delta v-2\mathcal{W}_{x}\wedge v_{y}-2v_{x}\wedge\mathcal{W}_{y}=0

for
\displaystyle v\in\mathcal{X}
. Here the first subscript
\displaystyle k
stands for the Fourier mode using complex notation, and the second subscript
\displaystyle j
is to distinguish two independent kernel functions in each mode. The explicit expressions of the infinitesimal generators (
1.10
) of rigid motions will be rather important in the analysis later as they will be projected onto the disk
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
due to the presence of boundary.

In the perturbative process, a key ingredient is the
\displaystyle L^{\infty}
-nondegeneracy of
\displaystyle L_{\mathcal{W}}
, which is one of the crucial elements in the construction of Chanillo-Malchiodi
[
14
]
in the case of degree
\displaystyle\pm 1
. By isoperimetric inequality, Isobe
[
28
]
also proved earlier a non-degeneracy property for the energy functional (
1.7
). The non-degeneracy for higher degree
\displaystyle H
-bubble
\displaystyle\pi(z^{m})
with
\displaystyle|m|\geq 2
was conjectured to be true by Chanillo and Malchiodi and was confirmed only recently by Sire and the last three authors in
[
38
]
. Degenerate bubbles of a general form were shown recently to exist and were characterized by Guerra-Lamy-Zemas in
[
20
]
.
See also Lenzmann-Schikorra
[
31
]
, Sire-Wei-Zheng
[
39
]
, and Chen-Liu-Wei
[
15
]
for related non-degeneracy properties of half-harmonic maps and higher degree harmonic maps. From
[
38
]
with degree
\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{Z}
, there are
\displaystyle 4|m|+5
bounded kernel functions to the linearization around
\displaystyle H
-bubble
\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{(m)}(z)=\pi(z^{m})
, and in particular, the linearized problem (
1.11
) only admits bounded solutions as linear combination of
\displaystyle Z_{k,j}
,
\displaystyle k=0,\pm 1,\pm 2
,
\displaystyle j=1,2
in (
1.10
), and

(1.12)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\frac{2(x^{2}-y^{2})((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}-1)}{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+1)^{2}}\\
\frac{4xy((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}-1)}{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+1)^{2}}\\
\frac{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}-1)^{2}}{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+1)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}\frac{2(x^{2}-y^{2})^{2}}{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+1)^{2}}\\
\frac{4xy(x^{2}-y^{2})}{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+1)^{2}}\\
\frac{(x^{2}-y^{2})((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}-1)}{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+1)^{2}}\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}\frac{4xy(x^{2}-y^{2})}{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+1)^{2}}\\
\frac{8x^{2}y^{2}}{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+1)^{2}}\\
\frac{2xy((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}-1)}{((x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+1)^{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Here we note that the nondegeneracy also holds in the
\displaystyle D^{1,2}
sense, as can be readily verified in the precise expressions given in
[
38
, Section 3]
, where

D^{1,2}:=\left\{u\in L_{{\rm loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{3}):\|u\|_{D^{1,2}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}<+\infty\right\}.

To analyze the Dirichlet problem (
1.2
), we consider the projected bubble

P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}=\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}-\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p},

where
\displaystyle\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p}
is defined in (
2.4
).
\displaystyle P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}
is the element of
\displaystyle H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{3})
closest to
\displaystyle\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}
in the Dirichlet norm. We may write

(1.13)

u=\sum_{i=1}^{k}P\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}+w,

where
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{i}\in SO(3)
,
\displaystyle\mu_{i}>0
,
\displaystyle\xi^{(i)}=(\xi^{(i)}_{1},\xi^{(i)}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\displaystyle a_{i}=(a_{i,1},a_{i,2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\displaystyle p_{i}=(p_{i,1},p_{i,2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
for all
\displaystyle i
, and
\displaystyle w
is a perturbation term. Here we remark that the extra three kernel functions in (
1.12
) correspond to translation invariance in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, and these are not included in the ansatz (
1.13
) due to the presence of boundary.

Our main theorem gives another partial answer to the question raised by Brezis and Coron
[
4
]
in the case of degree 2.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup\cdots\cup S_{k}\}
be any configuration of unit spheres, each passing through the origin of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then there exist a sequence of real numbers
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}\to 0^{+}
, functions
\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{n}:{\partial}\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
and a sequence of
\displaystyle u_{n}
with form (
1.13
) solving the H-system (
1.2
) on the unit disk
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
with boundary datum
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}\tilde{g}_{n}
such that the image of
\displaystyle u_{n}
converges to
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
in the Hausdorff sense.

The limiting configuration of the spheres,
e.g.

\displaystyle k=5
, is sketched in Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
The configuration of 5 spheres (for clearity, we use circles instead), each passing through the origin. As
\displaystyle\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, the image of the solution map covers each sphere twice.

The proof is based on the finite-dimensional reduction method together with the nondegeneracy property of the degree
\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle H
-bubble. See also Caldiroli-Musina
[
9
]
and Felli
[
19
]
for a related problem with perturbation in the mean curvature. Compared to the degree
\displaystyle 1
case in
[
14
]
, one of the main difficulties in the construction of Theorem
1.1
is the dealing of the extra bounded kernel functions in the linearization. The presence of these extra invariances results in the requirement of adjusting corresponding parameters in such a way that a critical point of the energy functional can be found. In particular, elaborate analysis is devoted to finding the extra four parameters
\displaystyle a_{1}
,
\displaystyle a_{2}
,
\displaystyle p_{1}
and
\displaystyle p_{2}
in (
1.9
) for each of the
\displaystyle k
bubbles in the degree 2 case. These parameters interact in the energy expansion through different combinations of kernel functions and their projections, and careful and delicate analysis is needed to make the interactions between different parameters precise. In fact, due to the Dirichlet boundary, each term in the approximation has to be projected. These projections enter the energy expansion as different Robin functions, whose explicit expressions depend on the bubble (
1.8
) and kernel functions (
1.10
). We derive in Lemma
2.2
and rely crucially on the explicit Robin functions to find the critical points of the reduced energy. In this process, several cancellation properties in the expansion are rather important.

One of the key steps in the proof is the choice of the boundary data. As a building block, we find a non-trivial degree 2 analogue of the harmonic extension compared to
[
36
,
14
]
; see Lemma
2.1
, in which its explicit form plays a rather important role in canceling out several large terms, measured in
\displaystyle\varepsilon
, in the energy expansion.
In the process of gluing
\displaystyle k
single bubbles, up to rotations, we choose
\displaystyle g
as a linear combination of the harmonic extension for the boundary condition

\tilde{g}_{\rho}(x,y)=2\left(\frac{(x-\rho)^{2}-y^{2}}{\left((x-\rho)^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}},\frac{2\left(x-\rho\right)y}{\left((x-\rho)^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}},\frac{-\varepsilon}{\left((x-\rho)^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}}\right)

for
\displaystyle(x,y)\in\partial\mathcal{D}
,
\displaystyle\rho\in(0,1)
is a parameter to be chosen. The motivation of such choice is to balance the leading part of the projection for
\displaystyle\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},0,0}
, assuming that the parameters
\displaystyle a_{i}
,
\displaystyle p_{i}
are suitably small. An important observation is that when the concentration points are sufficiently close to the boundary (
\displaystyle\rho
is close to 1), the interaction between the boundary datum and the bubbles will be stronger than the interaction among different bubbles. This near-boundary concentration is pictured in Figure
2
, where the smallness in the distance
\displaystyle d=1-\rho
also serves a crucial role when solving for the parameters
\displaystyle a_{i}
and
\displaystyle p_{i}
.
By choosing carefully
\displaystyle d
,
we capture the desired asymptotics of
\displaystyle a_{i}
and
\displaystyle p_{i}
measured in terms of
\displaystyle\varepsilon
; see (
2.7
), (
2.8
) and more detailed discussions in Section
2
.

The proof in this paper suggests that a similar (and much more complicated) construction might work for even higher degrees,
\displaystyle|m|\geq 3
. The core part reflects in the dealing of more parameters as degree increases.
On the other hand, the construction of a more general configuration that not all the unit spheres pass through the origin in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
seems to be more involved, where the ansatz of concentration pattern needs to be modified significantly. This will be the main object of a future work.

The rest of this paper is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.1
. In Section
2
, we introduce some preliminary facts, including the properties of the Robin function with degree 2 boundary condition and some basic integral and pointwise estimates for the degree 2
\displaystyle H
-bubble. In Section
3
, we reduce the problem into finding critical points of a function of the parameters using the finite-dimensional reduction method. To this aim, in Section
4
, we compute the expansion of energy functional
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)
for one bubble. In Section
5
, we expand the energy functional in the case of multiple bubbles. We postpone part of the lengthy but necessary computations in Appendices
A
and
B
. In Section
6
, we give the proof of Theorem
1.1
. In Appendix
C
, we provide a general method for computing the Robin function based on the Poisson integral formula and proving Lemma
2.2
. This general approach might be of independent interest and useful in further studies of higher degree harmonic maps and
\displaystyle H
-bubbles.

Notations.
Here we list some notations used throughout this paper.

(1)

We write
\displaystyle z=(x,y)
and identify the vector
\displaystyle z
as a complex number.

(2)

We use the symbol “
\displaystyle\lesssim
” to denote the inequality “
\displaystyle\leq C
” for a positive constant
\displaystyle C
that is independent of
\displaystyle\varepsilon
, while the constant
\displaystyle C
may vary from line to line.

(3)

Write
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}=\left(\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)}_{1},\xi^{(i)}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{i},a_{i,1},a_{i,2},p_{i,1},p_{i,2}\right)
, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\left(\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}},\cdots,\mathcal{A}_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\right)
.

(4)

\displaystyle\xi^{(i)}=\left(\xi^{(i)}_{1},\xi^{(i)}_{2}\right)
,
\displaystyle a_{i}=(a_{i,1},a_{i,2})
,
\displaystyle p_{i}=(p_{i,1},p_{i,2})
.

(5)

\displaystyle Pf=f-f_{0}
denotes the projection of
\displaystyle f
onto
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
,
i.e.
,
\displaystyle f_{0}
is the harmonic function that satisfies
\displaystyle f_{0}=f
on
\displaystyle\partial\mathcal{D}
.

2.
Preliminary facts

In this section, we introduce some preliminary facts. Let
\displaystyle\bar{I}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be defined in (
1.7
). As
\displaystyle\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, the functional
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}
is a perturbation of
\displaystyle\bar{I}
and we will find a critical point of
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}
by the finite-dimensional reduction method. The critical points of the functional
\displaystyle\bar{I}
will play a critical role in our construction, namely, the solutions of

(2.1)

\Delta u=2u_{x}\wedge u_{y}\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad u\in\mathcal{X}.

Observe that
\displaystyle\pi(z^{m})
is a solution of (
2.1
), which we call degree
\displaystyle m

\displaystyle H
-bubble.

For
\displaystyle m=2
, (
1.5
) is indeed a solution of (
2.1
). By invariance, the function given in (
1.6
) is also a solution of (
2.1
). Define

\bar{Z}=\left\{\mathcal{Q}\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}(z)=\mathcal{Q}\pi\left(\frac{\left(\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}-a\left|\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}}{\left(1-a\cdot\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}+|a|^{2}\left|\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}}+p\right)\Bigg|\ \mu>0,\ \xi,\ a,\ p\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\ \mathcal{Q}\in SO(3)\right\}.

We now list some useful expressions. For
\displaystyle\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}
, we have the following expansion,

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}:=\delta_{\mu,\xi}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}+\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}},

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}:=\sum_{l=1}^{2}\left(a_{l}Z_{-1,l}+p_{l}Z_{2,l}\right)
is the linear part,
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
denotes the higher order terms. These higher order terms include, for example, the following expressions

a_{j}a_{k}Z^{-1,-1}_{j,k}:=a_{j}a_{k}\frac{{\partial}^{2}\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}}{{\partial}a_{j}{\partial}a_{k}}\Bigg|_{a=0,p=0},\ a_{j}p_{k}Z^{-1,2}_{j,k}:=a_{j}p_{k}\frac{{\partial}^{2}\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}}{{\partial}a_{j}{\partial}p_{k}}\Bigg|_{a=0,p=0},\ p_{j}p_{k}Z^{2,2}_{j,k}:=p_{j}p_{k}\frac{{\partial}^{2}\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}}{{\partial}p_{j}{\partial}p_{k}}\Bigg|_{a=0,p=0}.

From (
1.8
), (
1.9
) and (
1.10
), if
\displaystyle|z-\xi|\geq c>0
, for
\displaystyle\mu
small and
\displaystyle a_{i}=o(\mu)
,
\displaystyle p_{i}=o(\mu)
,
\displaystyle i=1,2
, the following expansion holds in the case
\displaystyle m=2
,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu,\xi}(z)

\displaystyle\sim\left(\frac{2\mu^{2}\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{2}-(y-\xi_{2})^{2}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}},\frac{4\mu^{2}(x-\xi_{1})(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}},1-\frac{2\mu^{4}}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}}\right)+O(\mu^{5}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)

\displaystyle\sim a_{1}\left(\frac{-4\mu(x-\xi_{1})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{2}},\frac{-4\mu(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{2}},\frac{8\mu^{3}(x-\xi_{1})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}}\right)+a_{1}O(\mu^{5})

\displaystyle+a_{2}\left(\frac{4\mu(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{2}},\frac{-4\mu(x-\xi_{1})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{2}},\frac{8\mu^{3}(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}}\right)+a_{2}O(\mu^{5})

\displaystyle+p_{1}\left(\frac{-2\mu^{4}\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{4}-6(x-\xi_{1})^{2}(y-\xi_{2})^{2}+(y-\xi_{2})^{4}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}},\right.

\displaystyle\left.\frac{-8\mu^{4}(x-\xi_{1})(y-\xi_{2})\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{2}-(y-\xi_{2})^{2}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}},\frac{4\mu^{6}\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{2}-(y-\xi_{2})^{2}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}}\right)+p_{1}O(\mu^{7})

\displaystyle+p_{2}\left(\frac{-8\mu^{4}(x-\xi_{1})(y-\xi_{2})\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{2}-(y-\xi_{2})^{2}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}},\right.

\displaystyle\left.\frac{2\mu^{4}\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{4}-6(x-\xi_{1})^{2}(y-\xi_{2})^{2}+(y-\xi_{2})^{4}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}},\frac{8\mu^{6}(x-\xi_{1})(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}}\right)+p_{2}O(\mu^{7}).

The terms in
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)
can also be expanded in a similar manner, but we omit the details here.

Let
\displaystyle\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the unique solution of the following problem

(2.3)

\begin{cases}\Delta\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p}=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p}=\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D},\end{cases}

and set
\displaystyle P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}=\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}-\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p}
. The regular part
\displaystyle\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p}
can be expressed as

\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p}:=\varphi_{1}+a_{1}\varphi_{-1,1}+a_{2}\varphi_{-1,2}+p_{1}\varphi_{2,1}+p_{2}\varphi_{2,2}+\varphi_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}},

here
\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}}
denotes the regular part associated with
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Using (
2.2
), we obtain the following expansion

(2.4)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(z,\xi)=

\displaystyle\left(2\mu^{2}h_{1}^{(1)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{6}),2\mu^{2}h_{2}^{(1)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{6}),1-2\mu^{4}h_{3}^{(1)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{8})\right),

\displaystyle\varphi_{-1,1}(z,\xi)=

\displaystyle\left(2\mu h_{1}^{(-1,1)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{5}),2\mu h_{2}^{(-1,1)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{5}),-2\mu^{3}h_{3}^{(-1,1)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{7})\right),

\displaystyle\varphi_{-1,2}(z,\xi)=

\displaystyle\left(2\mu h_{1}^{(-1,2)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{5}),2\mu h_{2}^{(-1,2)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{5}),-2\mu^{3}h_{3}^{(-1,2)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{7})\right),

\displaystyle\varphi_{2,1}(z,\xi)=

\displaystyle\left(2\mu^{4}h_{1}^{(2,1)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{8}),2\mu^{4}h_{2}^{(2,1)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{8}),-2\mu^{6}h_{3}^{(2,1)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{10})\right),

\displaystyle\varphi_{2,2}(z,\xi)=

\displaystyle\left(2\mu^{4}h_{1}^{(2,2)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{8}),2\mu^{4}h_{2}^{(2,2)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{8}),-2\mu^{6}h_{3}^{(2,2)}(z,\xi)+O(\mu^{10})\right).

For given
\displaystyle(z,\xi)=((x,y),(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}))\in\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}
,
\displaystyle h_{1}^{(1)}
,
\displaystyle h_{2}^{(1)}
,
\displaystyle h_{3}^{(1)}:\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are the solutions to the following problems with boundary data determined by the explicit profile of degree
\displaystyle 2
,

(2.5)

\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}h_{1}^{(1)}(z,\xi)=0,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \Delta_{z}h_{2}^{(1)}(z,\xi)=0,\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\Delta_{z}h_{3}^{(1)}(z,\xi)=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
h_{1}^{(1)}(z,\xi)=\frac{(x-\xi_{1})^{2}-(y-\xi_{2})^{2}}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}},\quad h_{2}^{(1)}(z,\xi)=\frac{2(x-\xi_{1})(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}},\quad h_{3}^{(1)}(z,\xi)=\frac{1}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D}.\end{cases}

And
\displaystyle h_{i}^{(-1,1)}
,
\displaystyle h_{i}^{(-1,2)}
,
\displaystyle h_{i}^{(2,1)}
,
\displaystyle h_{i}^{(2,2)}:\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\displaystyle i=1,2,3
are the solutions of the following problems,

\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}h_{1}^{(-1,1)}(z,\xi)=0,\qquad\qquad\quad\Delta_{z}h_{2}^{(-1,1)}(z,\xi)=0,\qquad\qquad\quad\Delta_{z}h_{3}^{(-1,1)}(z,\xi)=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
h_{1}^{(-1,1)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-2(x-\xi_{1})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{2}},\qquad h_{2}^{(-1,1)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-2(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{2}},\qquad h_{3}^{(-1,1)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-4(x-\xi_{1})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D},\end{cases}

\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}h_{1}^{(-1,2)}(z,\xi)=0,\qquad\qquad\ \Delta_{z}h_{2}^{(-1,2)}(z,\xi)=0,\qquad\qquad\quad\Delta_{z}h_{3}^{(-1,2)}(z,\xi)=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
h_{1}^{(-1,2)}(z,\xi)=\frac{2(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{2}},\qquad h_{2}^{(-1,2)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-2(x-\xi_{1})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{2}},\qquad h_{3}^{(-1,2)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-4(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{4}}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}h_{1}^{(2,1)}(z,\xi)=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
h_{1}^{(2,1)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{4}-6(x-\xi_{1})^{2}(y-\xi_{2})^{2}+(y-\xi_{2})^{4}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}h_{2}^{(2,1)}(z,\xi)=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
h_{2}^{(2,1)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-4(x-\xi_{1})(y-\xi_{2})\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{2}-(y-\xi_{2})^{2}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}h_{3}^{(2,1)}(z,\xi)=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
h_{3}^{(2,1)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-2\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{2}-(y-\xi_{2})^{2}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}h_{1}^{(2,2)}(z,\xi)=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
h_{1}^{(2,2)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-4(x-\xi_{1})(y-\xi_{2})\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{2}-(y-\xi_{2})^{2}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}h_{2}^{(2,2)}(z,\xi)=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
h_{2}^{(2,2)}(z,\xi)=\frac{\left[(x-\xi_{1})^{4}-6(x-\xi_{1})^{2}(y-\xi_{2})^{2}+(y-\xi_{2})^{4}\right]}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}h_{3}^{(2,2)}(z,\xi)=0~&\text{in}\ \mathcal{D},\\
h_{3}^{(2,2)}(z,\xi)=\frac{-4(x-\xi_{1})(y-\xi_{2})}{\left|z-\xi\right|^{8}}~&\text{on}\ \partial\mathcal{D}.\end{cases}

It is standard to deduce from (
1.8
), (
1.10
) and (
2.4
) that

(2.6)

\displaystyle P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}=\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}-\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu,\xi}\left(z\right)-\varphi_{1}(z,\xi)+\sum_{l=1}^{2}a_{l}\left[Z_{-1,l}\left(\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right)-\varphi_{-1,l}(z,\xi)\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{2}p_{l}\left[Z_{2,l}\left(\frac{z-\xi}{\mu}\right)-\varphi_{2,l}(z,\xi)\right]+\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}-\varphi_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}}.

To present our results, we require additional information about
\displaystyle\varphi_{\mu,\xi,a,p}
. In
[
14
]
, let
\displaystyle G(z,\xi)
be the Green’s function of
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
that satisfies
\displaystyle G(z,\xi)\sim-\log|z-\xi|
for
\displaystyle z\sim\xi
. Denoting the regular part of
\displaystyle G(z,\xi)
as
\displaystyle H(z,\xi)
, then
\displaystyle G(z,\xi)=-\log|z-\xi|-H(z,\xi)
and

h^{(-1,1)}_{1}(z,\xi)=h^{(-1,2)}_{2}(z,\xi)=-2\frac{\partial H(z,\xi)}{\partial\xi_{1}},\quad h^{(-1,1)}_{2}(z,\xi)=-h^{(-1,2)}_{1}(z,\xi)=-2\frac{\partial H(z,\xi)}{\partial\xi_{2}}.

And from
[
14
, Proposition 6.1]
, we know that

\frac{\partial h^{(-1,1)}_{1}}{\partial x}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{{\partial}h^{(-1,1)}_{2}}{\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial h^{(-1,2)}_{2}}{\partial x}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial h^{(-1,2)}_{1}}{\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{-2}{\left(1-|\xi|^{2}\right)^{2}}.

Nontrivial observations are the following relations,

\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}h_{2}^{(-1,1)}}{{\partial}\xi_{2}}(z,\xi)=2h^{(1)}_{1}(z,\xi),\qquad-\frac{{\partial}h_{1}^{(-1,1)}}{{\partial}\xi_{2}}(z,\xi)=2h^{(1)}_{2}(z,\xi).

Then we have the following result.

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume
\displaystyle\xi\in\mathcal{D}
,
\displaystyle\tilde{h}(\cdot,\xi):\partial\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the function defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{h}(z,\xi)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{(x-\xi_{1})^{2}-(y-\xi_{2})^{2}}{\left(\left(x-\xi_{1}\right)^{2}+(y-\xi_{2})^{2}\right)^{2}},\frac{2\left(x-\xi_{1}\right)\left(y-\xi_{2}\right)}{\left(\left(x-\xi_{1}\right)^{2}+(y-\xi_{2})^{2}\right)^{2}},0\right),\quad z=(x,y)\in\partial\mathcal{D}.

Then the harmonic extension on
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
of
\displaystyle\tilde{h}(z,\xi)
is given by

\displaystyle h(z,\xi)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\left((\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)-x+y\right)\left((\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)-x-y\right)}{\left(\left(\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\right)\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)-2\xi_{1}x-2\xi_{2}y+1\right)^{2}},\right.

\displaystyle\left.\frac{2\left(\xi_{1}(x^{2}+y^{2})-x\right)\left(\xi_{2}\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)-y\right)}{\left(\left(\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\right)\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)-2\xi_{1}x-2\xi_{2}y+1\right)^{2}},0\right),\quad z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}.

Note that
\displaystyle h(z,\xi)=\left(h^{(1)}_{1}(z,\xi),h^{(1)}_{2}(z,\xi),0\right)
. Now we can compute the values of the Robin functions
\displaystyle h^{(-1,l)}_{1}(\xi,\xi)
,
\displaystyle h^{(-1,l)}_{2}(\xi,\xi)
,
\displaystyle h^{(1)}_{1}(\xi,\xi)
,
\displaystyle h^{(1)}_{2}(\xi,\xi)
,
\displaystyle h^{(2,l)}_{1}(\xi,\xi)
,
\displaystyle h^{(2,l)}_{2}(\xi,\xi)
,
\displaystyle l=1,2
.

Lemma 2.2
.

For the Robin functions
\displaystyle h_{1}^{(-1,l)}(\xi,\xi)
,
\displaystyle h_{2}^{(-1,l)}(\xi,\xi)
,
\displaystyle l=1,2
, one has

\displaystyle h_{1}^{(-1,1)}(\xi,\xi)=h_{2}^{(-1,2)}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{-2\xi_{1}}{1-|\xi|^{2}},\quad h_{2}^{(-1,1)}(\xi,\xi)=-h_{1}^{(-1,2)}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{-2\xi_{2}}{1-|\xi|^{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial h_{1}^{(-1,1)}}{\partial x}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial h_{2}^{(-1,1)}}{\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{-2}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{2}},\qquad\frac{\partial h_{1}^{(-1,1)}}{\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial h_{2}^{(-1,1)}}{\partial x}(\xi,\xi)=0,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial h_{1}^{(-1,2)}}{\partial x}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial h_{2}^{(-1,2)}}{\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=0,\qquad\ \frac{\partial h_{1}^{(-1,2)}}{\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial h_{2}^{(-1,2)}}{\partial x}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{2}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{2}},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}h_{1}^{(-1,1)}}{\partial x^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{2}h_{1}^{(-1,1)}}{\partial y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{4\xi_{1}}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{3}},\quad\ \frac{\partial^{2}h_{2}^{(-1,1)}}{\partial x^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{2}h_{2}^{(-1,1)}}{\partial y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{4\xi_{2}}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{3}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}h_{1}^{(-1,2)}}{\partial x^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{2}h_{1}^{(-1,2)}}{\partial y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{4\xi_{2}}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{3}},\quad\ \frac{\partial^{2}h_{2}^{(-1,2)}}{\partial x^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{2}h_{2}^{(-1,2)}}{\partial y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{4\xi_{1}}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{3}}.

For the functions
\displaystyle h_{1}^{(1)}(z,\xi)
and
\displaystyle h_{2}^{(1)}(z,\xi)
, we have the following identities

\displaystyle\displaystyle h_{1}^{(1)}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2}}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{2}},\quad h_{2}^{(1)}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{2\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{2}},\quad\frac{\partial h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{2\xi_{1}}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{3}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial x}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{2\xi_{2}}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{3}},\qquad\frac{\partial^{2}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial^{2}h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial x{\partial}y}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{2(1+2|\xi|^{2})}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{4}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{2}h_{2}^{(1)}}{{\partial}x\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{-2(1+2|\xi|^{2})}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{4}},\quad\frac{\partial^{2}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{2}h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial^{2}h_{2}^{(1)}}{{\partial}y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=0,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{3}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{3}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial^{3}h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{2}{\partial}y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{3}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x{\partial}y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{3}h_{2}^{(1)}}{{\partial}y^{3}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{12\xi_{1}\left(1+|\xi|^{2}\right)}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{5}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{3}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial y^{3}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{3}h_{2}^{(1)}}{{\partial}x\partial y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{3}h_{1}^{(1)}}{{\partial}x^{2}\partial y}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial^{3}h_{2}^{(1)}}{{\partial}x^{3}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{-12\xi_{2}\left(1+|\xi|^{2}\right)}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{5}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{4}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial^{4}h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{3}{\partial}y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{4}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{2}{\partial}y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{4}h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial x{\partial}y^{3}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial^{4}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial y^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{24(\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2})(3+2|\xi|^{2})}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{6}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{4}h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{4}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{3}{\partial}y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{4}h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{2}{\partial}y^{2}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial^{4}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x{\partial}y^{3}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{\partial^{4}h_{2}^{(1)}}{\partial y^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{-48\xi_{1}\xi_{2}(3+2|\xi|^{2})}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{6}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{6}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial x^{6}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{\partial^{6}h_{1}^{(1)}}{\partial y^{6}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{720\left(2\xi_{1}^{6}+\left(5-10\xi_{2}^{2}\right)\xi_{1}^{4}-10\xi_{2}^{2}\left(\xi_{2}^{2}+3\right)\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{4}\left(2\xi_{2}^{2}+5\right)\right)}{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{8}}.

For the functions
\displaystyle h^{(2,l)}
,
\displaystyle l=1,2
, one has

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}h_{1}^{(2,1)}}{{\partial}x}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{{\partial}h_{2}^{(2,1)}}{{\partial}y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{4\xi_{1}\left(\xi_{1}^{2}-3\xi_{2}^{2}\right)}{\left(1-|\xi|^{2}\right)^{5}},\qquad\frac{{\partial}h_{1}^{(2,1)}}{{\partial}y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{{\partial}h_{2}^{(2,1)}}{{\partial}x}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{4\xi_{2}\left(-3\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\right)}{\left(1-|\xi|^{2}\right)^{5}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}^{4}h_{1}^{(2,1)}}{{\partial}x^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{{\partial}^{4}h_{1}^{(2,1)}}{{\partial}y^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{24\left(35+4\left(|\xi|^{2}-1\right)^{3}+30\left(|\xi|^{2}-1\right)^{2}+60\left(|\xi|^{2}-1\right)\right)}{\left(1-|\xi|^{2}\right)^{8}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}^{4}h_{2}^{(2,1)}}{{\partial}x^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{{\partial}^{4}h_{2}^{(2,1)}}{{\partial}y^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=0,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}h_{1}^{(2,2)}}{{\partial}x}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{{\partial}h_{2}^{(2,2)}}{{\partial}y}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{4\xi_{2}\left(-3\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\right)}{\left(1-|\xi|^{2}\right)^{5}},\qquad\frac{{\partial}h_{1}^{(2,2)}}{{\partial}y}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{{\partial}h_{2}^{(2,2)}}{{\partial}x}(\xi,\xi)=-\frac{4\xi_{1}\left(\xi_{1}^{2}-3\xi_{2}^{2}\right)}{\left(1-|\xi|^{2}\right)^{5}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}^{4}h_{1}^{(2,2)}}{{\partial}x^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{{\partial}^{4}h_{1}^{(2,2)}}{{\partial}y^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=0,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}^{4}h_{2}^{(2,2)}}{{\partial}x^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{{\partial}^{4}h_{2}^{(2,2)}}{{\partial}y^{4}}(\xi,\xi)=\frac{24\left(35+4\left(|\xi|^{2}-1\right)^{3}+30\left(|\xi|^{2}-1\right)^{2}+60\left(|\xi|^{2}-1\right)\right)}{\left(1-|\xi|^{2}\right)^{8}}.

In Appendix
C
, we provide the proof of Lemma
2.2
using the Poisson integral formula. This approach does not depend on the explicit forms of the Robin functions. We believe it will be valuable for future studies on harmonic maps and
\displaystyle H
-bubbles of any degree.

Let us set up the problem as follows. For
\displaystyle\mu
sufficiently small, we define

(2.7)

d=d_{\varepsilon}=1-|\xi|^{2}=O\left(\mu^{\frac{2}{3}-\alpha}\right),

where
\displaystyle\alpha
is a small but fixed positive constant. Given a positive integer
\displaystyle k
, we place
\displaystyle k
points on a circle centered at the origin with radius
\displaystyle 1-d
, and they are parametrized as follows,

\left((1-d)\cos\frac{2\pi j}{k},(1-d)\sin\frac{2\pi j}{k}\right),\quad j=1,2,\dots,k.

The locations of bubbles are visualized as in Figure
2
below.

Figure 2.
The approximate multiple spikes are located at
\displaystyle k
given points, which are equidistributed on a circle of radius
\displaystyle 1-d
with center at origin,
\displaystyle k=5
for example.

As we will see in Subsection
5.4
, the main orders of the parameters are

(2.8)

\mu_{0}=\sqrt{\varepsilon},\quad a_{1}\sim\sqrt{\varepsilon}d^{2},\quad a_{2}\sim\sqrt{\varepsilon}d^{2},\quad p_{1}\sim\frac{d^{5}}{\varepsilon},\quad p_{2}\sim\frac{d^{5}}{\varepsilon}.

We conclude this section with some estimates for further use. Let
\displaystyle\tau=\frac{d}{2}
and
\displaystyle B_{\tau}:=B_{\tau}(\xi)
be the ball of radius
\displaystyle\tau
centered at
\displaystyle\xi
. Then for all
\displaystyle z\in\mathcal{D},

\displaystyle l=1,2
, we have

(2.9)

\displaystyle\|P\delta\|_{L^{1}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu^{2}\left|\log\mu\right|\right),\quad\|\nabla P\delta\|_{L^{1}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu\right),\quad\|P\delta\|_{L^{2}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu\right),\quad\|\nabla P\delta\|_{L^{2}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(1\right),

\displaystyle\|P\delta\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash B_{\tau}\right)}+\|\nabla P\delta\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu^{2}d^{-2}\right),\quad|\varphi_{1}-(0,0,1)|(z)=O\left(\mu^{2}d^{-2}\right),\quad|\nabla\varphi_{1}|(z)=O\left(\mu^{2}d^{-3}\right),

(2.10)

\displaystyle\|PZ_{-1,l}\|_{L^{1}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu d\right),\quad\|\nabla PZ_{-1,l}\|_{L^{1}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu\left|\log\mu\right|\right),\quad\|PZ_{-1,l}\|_{L^{2}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu\left|\log\mu\right|\right),

\displaystyle\|PZ_{-1,l}\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash B_{\tau}\right)}+\|\nabla PZ_{-1,l}\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu d^{-1}\right),\quad|\varphi_{-1,l}|(z)=O(\mu d^{-1}),\quad|\nabla\varphi_{-1,l}|(z)=O(\mu d^{-2}).

(2.11)

\displaystyle\|PZ_{2,l}\|_{L^{1}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu^{2}\right),\quad\|\nabla PZ_{2,l}\|_{L^{1}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu\right),\quad\|PZ_{2,l}\|_{L^{2}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu\right),

\displaystyle\|PZ_{2,l}\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash B_{\tau}\right)}+\|\nabla PZ_{2,l}\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu^{4}d^{-4}\right),\quad|\varphi_{2,l}|(z)=O(\mu^{4}d^{-4}),\quad|\nabla\varphi_{2,l}|(z)=O(\mu^{4}d^{-5}),

(2.12)

\displaystyle\|P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\|_{L^{1}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu^{2}\left|\log\mu\right|\right),\quad\|\nabla P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\|_{L^{1}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu\right),\quad\|P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\|_{L^{2}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu\right),

\displaystyle\|\nabla P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\|_{L^{2}\left(B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(1\right),\quad\|P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash B_{\tau}\right)}+\|\nabla P\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash B_{\tau}\right)}=O\left(\mu^{2}d^{-2}\right).

3.
The finite-dimensional reduction and
\displaystyle C^{1}
-estimates

In this section, we employ the finite-dimensional reduction method to reduce the problem into a finite-dimensional one on the parameters
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q},\ \mu,\ \xi,\ a,\ p
.
Given
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\displaystyle\bar{C}>0
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{i}\in SO(3)
, set

(3.1)

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}P\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}\,\Bigg|\,\ \text{dist}\left(\xi^{(i)},\xi^{(j)}\right)\geq\bar{C}^{-1},\ \forall i\neq j,\ \frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mu_{i}},\ \frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{2}}{|a_{i}|},\ \frac{\varepsilon^{-1}d^{5}}{|p_{i}|}\in\left[\bar{C}^{-1},\bar{C}\right]\right\}.

We denote the elements in
\displaystyle Z
as
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
, where
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}=P\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}.
The following result is a direct consequence of the non-degeneracy of the degree 2
\displaystyle H
-bubble proved in
[
38
]
recently.

Proposition 3.1
.

There exists a constant
\displaystyle C_{0}>0
such that

\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\right)\left[Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p},Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\right]\leq-C_{0}\|\nabla(Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\quad\text{for all}\quad Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\in\bar{Z},

and

\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\right)\left[v,v\right]\geq C_{0}\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\quad\text{for all}\quad Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\in\bar{Z}\ \text{and}\ v\perp\left(T_{Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}}\bar{Z}\oplus\{tQ\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\}_{t}\right).

In particular,
\displaystyle\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}\right)\left[v\right]=0
implies
\displaystyle v-c\in T_{Q\delta_{\mu,\xi,a,p}}\bar{Z}
for some
\displaystyle c\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z
, there exists a positive constant
\displaystyle C
such that if
\displaystyle v\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})
,
\displaystyle v\perp T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z
,
\displaystyle v\perp z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}},\ \forall i
, then

I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\left[v,v\right]\geq C^{-1}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}^{2}.

Proof.

For
\displaystyle i=1,\dots,k
, let
\displaystyle B_{i}
and
\displaystyle\tilde{B}_{i}
be the balls of radius
\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}
and
\displaystyle\frac{d}{4}
centered at
\displaystyle\xi^{(i)}
, respectively. For any
\displaystyle v\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})
, set

(3.2)

v_{1}=v-\sum_{i=1}^{k}P_{i}v,

where
\displaystyle P_{i}
is the orthogonal projection of
\displaystyle H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})
onto
\displaystyle H_{0}^{1}(B_{i})
. It follows that

(3.3)

\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|P_{i}v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}^{2}+\|v_{1}\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}^{2}.

Since
\displaystyle v_{1}
is harmonic in each
\displaystyle B_{i}
and coincides with
\displaystyle v
on
\displaystyle\partial B_{i}
, by standard elliptic regularity theory, we have

(3.4)

\|v_{1}\|_{C^{2}(\tilde{B}_{i})}\leq C\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})},\quad\forall~i=1,\dots,k,

where
\displaystyle C
is a constant independent of
\displaystyle v
.

Now we compute
\displaystyle(P_{i}v,z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})
. Using (
3.2
), we have

(P_{i}v,z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})=\left(v-v_{1}-\sum_{i\neq j}^{k}P_{i}v,z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)=-(v_{1},z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})+\left(v-\sum_{i\neq j}^{k}P_{i}v,z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right).

Since
\displaystyle v
is orthogonal to
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
, from (
2.12
), and using Hölder’s inequality, we deduce

\displaystyle\left(v-\sum_{j\neq i}P_{j}v,z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)=-\left(\sum_{j\neq i}P_{j}v,z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)=-\sum_{j\neq i}\int_{B_{j}}\nabla P_{j}v\cdot\nabla z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}=O(\mu_{i}^{2})\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Additionally, by using (
2.12
) and (
3.4
), we have

\displaystyle(v_{1},z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}}\nabla v_{1}\cdot\nabla z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\tilde{B}_{i}}\nabla v_{1}\cdot\nabla z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}+\int_{\mathcal{D}\backslash\tilde{B}_{i}}\nabla v_{1}\cdot\nabla z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\|_{L^{1}{\left(\tilde{B}_{i}\right)}}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}+\|\nabla z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\|\|_{L^{2}{\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash\tilde{B}_{i}\right)}}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}

\displaystyle=O(\mu_{i})\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}+O(\mu_{i}^{2}d^{-2})\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Combine the above estimates, and recall that
\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mu_{i}}\in\left[\bar{C}^{-1},\bar{C}\right]
,
\displaystyle d=d_{\varepsilon}=1-|\xi^{(i)}|^{2}=O\left(\mu_{i}^{\frac{2}{3}-\alpha}\right)
, where
\displaystyle\alpha
is a small but fixed positive constant, we obtain

(3.5)

|(P_{i}v,z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})|=O(\mu_{i}^{2}d^{-2})\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}\leq C\varepsilon d^{-2}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Next, we evaluate
\displaystyle\left(P_{i}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right)
. Similarly,

\left(P_{i}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right)=\left(v-v_{1}-\sum_{j\neq i}P_{j}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right)=-\left(v_{1},\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right)+\left(v-\sum_{j\neq i}P_{j}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right).

Since
\displaystyle v
is orthogonal to
\displaystyle\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}
, we deduce from (
2.10
) that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\left(v-\sum_{j\neq i}P_{j}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\left(\sum_{j\neq i}P_{j}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\sum_{j\neq i}\int_{B_{j}}\nabla P_{j}v\cdot\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{j\neq i}\left\|\nabla\left(P\mathcal{Q}_{i}Z_{-1,1}\left(\frac{z-\xi^{(i)}}{\mu_{i}}\right)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}{\left(B_{j}\right)}}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=O(\mu_{i})\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Furthermore, using (
2.10
) and (
3.4
), we have

\displaystyle\left(v_{1},\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}}\nabla v_{1}\cdot\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\tilde{B}_{i}}\nabla v_{1}\cdot\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}+\int_{\mathcal{D}\backslash\tilde{B}_{i}}\nabla v_{1}\cdot\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right\|_{L^{1}{\left(\tilde{B}_{i}\right)}}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}+\left\|\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right\|_{L^{2}{\left(\mathcal{D}\backslash\tilde{B}_{i}\right)}}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}

\displaystyle=O\left(\mu_{i}|\log\mu_{i}|\right)\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}+O(\mu_{i}d^{-1})\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Thus, we obtain

\left|\left(P_{i}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}\right)\right|\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{-1}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

For
\displaystyle\left(P_{i}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}\right)
, using the orthogonality between
\displaystyle v
and
\displaystyle\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}
, along with the estimates provided in (
2.11
) and (
3.4
), there holds

\displaystyle\left(P_{i}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(v-v_{1}-\sum_{j\neq i}P_{j}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(v_{1},\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}\right)+\left(v-\sum_{j\neq i}P_{j}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{D}}\nabla v_{1}\cdot\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}-\left(\sum_{j\neq i}P_{j}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\tilde{B}_{i}}\nabla v_{1}\cdot\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}-\int_{\mathcal{D}\backslash\tilde{B}_{i}}\nabla v_{1}\cdot\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}-\sum_{j\neq i}\int_{B_{j}}\nabla P_{j}v\cdot\nabla\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}

\displaystyle=O(\mu_{i})\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}+O(\mu_{i}^{4}d^{-4})\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Thus

\left|\left(P_{i}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}\right)\right|\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Similarly, we can deduce

\left|\left(P_{i}v,\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial Q_{\alpha_{i}}}+\mu_{i}\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial\mu_{i}}+\mu_{i}\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial\xi^{(i)}_{1}}+\mu_{i}\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial\xi^{(i)}_{2}}+\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,2}}+\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,2}}\right)\right|\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{-1}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Let
\displaystyle\Pi_{i}
denote the orthogonal projection onto the space spanned by
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
,
\displaystyle\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{i}}
,
\displaystyle\mu_{i}\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial\mu_{i}}
,
\displaystyle\mu_{i}\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial\xi^{(i)}_{1}}
,
\displaystyle\mu_{i}\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial\xi^{(i)}_{2}}
,
\displaystyle\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,1}}
,
\displaystyle\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial a_{i,2}}
,
\displaystyle\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,1}}
and
\displaystyle\frac{\partial z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}}{\partial p_{i,2}}
. Then we obtain

\|\Pi_{i}P_{i}v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{-1}\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

The functional
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)
is given by

I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[v,\tilde{v}]=\int_{\mathcal{D}}\nabla v\cdot\nabla\tilde{v}+2\int_{\mathcal{D}}z_{\mathcal{A}}\cdot(v_{x}\wedge\tilde{v}_{y}+\tilde{v}_{x}\wedge v_{y})+2\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}}\tilde{v}\cdot(g_{x}\wedge v_{y}+v_{x}\wedge g_{y}),\quad v,\tilde{v}\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D}).

Hence, for an arbitrary function
\displaystyle v
, there holds

I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[v,v]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v,P_{i}v]+I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[v_{1},v_{1}]+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v,v_{1}].

For
\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v,P_{i}v]
, we have

I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v,P_{i}v]=\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v,P_{i}v]+2\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}}P_{i}v\cdot\left(g_{x}\wedge(P_{i}v)_{y}+(P_{i}v)_{x}\wedge g_{y}\right),

where
\displaystyle\bar{I}(u)
is the functional given in (
1.7
). Furthermore, we have

\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v,P_{i}v]=\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[\Pi_{i}P_{i}v,\Pi_{i}P_{i}v]+\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v-\Pi_{i}P_{i}v,P_{i}v-\Pi_{i}P_{i}v].

By Proposition
3.1
and (
3.5
), we have

\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[\Pi_{i}P_{i}v,\Pi_{i}P_{i}v]=\left(P_{i}v,z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)^{2}\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}},z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}]\leq-C\varepsilon^{2}d^{-4}\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})},

and

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{I}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v-\Pi_{i}P_{i}v,P_{i}v-\Pi_{i}P_{i}v]

\displaystyle\displaystyle\geq C_{0}\|\nabla P_{i}v-\nabla\Pi_{i}P_{i}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\geq C_{0}\left(\|\nabla P_{i}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}-\|\nabla\Pi_{i}P_{i}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\geq C_{0}\left(\|P_{i}v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}-\|\Pi_{i}P_{i}v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\geq C_{0}\left(\|P_{i}v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}-C\varepsilon d^{-2}\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}\right).

Moreover,

\left|\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}}P_{i}v\cdot[g_{x}\wedge(P_{i}v)_{y}+(P_{i}v)_{x}\wedge g_{y}]\right|\leq C\varepsilon\|P_{i}v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})},

then

(3.6)

I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v,P_{i}v]\geq C_{0}\|P_{i}v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}-C\varepsilon d^{-2}\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Next, we compute
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v,v_{1}]
. From the orthogonality of
\displaystyle P_{i}v
and
\displaystyle v_{1}
, we have

(3.7)

\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[P_{i}v,v_{1}]

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathcal{D}}z_{\mathcal{A}}\cdot[(P_{i}v)_{x}\wedge(v_{1})_{y}+(v_{1})_{x}\wedge(P_{i}v)_{y}]+2\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}}P_{i}v\cdot[g_{x}\wedge(v_{1})_{y}+(v_{1})_{x}\wedge g_{y}]

\displaystyle=O\left(\int_{\mathcal{D}}\left|z_{\mathcal{A}}\right||\nabla P_{i}v||\nabla v_{1}|\right)+O(\varepsilon)\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})},

and by (
2.12
), (
3.3
) and (
3.4
), we have

(3.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}}\left|z_{\mathcal{A}}\right||\nabla P_{i}v||\nabla v_{1}|

\displaystyle=\int_{\tilde{B}_{i}}\left|z_{\mathcal{A}}\right||\nabla P_{i}v||\nabla v_{1}|+\int_{\mathcal{D}\backslash\tilde{B}_{i}}\left|z_{\mathcal{A}}\right||\nabla P_{i}v||\nabla v_{1}|

\displaystyle=\|z_{\mathcal{A}}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{B}_{i})}\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}+\|z_{\mathcal{A}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}\backslash\tilde{B}_{i})}\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}

\displaystyle=O(\mu_{i})\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}+O(\mu_{i}^{2}d^{-2})\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[v_{1},v_{1}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}}|\nabla v_{1}|^{2}+2\int_{\mathcal{D}}z_{\mathcal{A}}\cdot[(v_{1})_{x}\wedge(v_{1})_{y}+(v_{1})_{x}\wedge(v_{1})_{y}]+2\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}}v_{1}\cdot[g_{x}\wedge(v_{1})_{y}+(v_{1})_{x}\wedge g_{y}]

(3.9)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\|v_{1}\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}+O(\mu_{i}^{2}d^{-2})\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}+O(\varepsilon)\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

From (
3.3
), (
3.6
), (
3.7
), (
3.8
) and (
3.9
), we can find a positive constant
\displaystyle C
such that

I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[v,v]\geq C^{-1}\|v\|^{2}_{H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D})}.

Thus, we complete the proof of Lemma
3.2
.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

If
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small, for every
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z
, there exists a function
\displaystyle w_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\in H^{1}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{3})
and a constant
\displaystyle C>0
such that for all
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z
,
\displaystyle w_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)
is orthogonal to
\displaystyle T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z
,
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}+w_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\right)\in T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z
and
\displaystyle\|w_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\|\leq C\|I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\|
. Therefore, the manifold

Z_{\varepsilon}=\{z_{\mathcal{A}}+w_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right):z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z\}

is a natural constraint for the functional
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
. That is to say, if
\displaystyle u\in Z_{\varepsilon}
and
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}|_{Z_{\varepsilon}}(u)=0
, then
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)=0
.

Proof.

The proof is standard, and we present it here for convenience; see also
[
14
]
. Let
\displaystyle q_{i}=q_{i}(z_{\mathcal{A}})
,
\displaystyle i=1,\dots,10
denote the orthogonal basis of
\displaystyle T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z
(note that there are three kernel functions corresponding to the rotation matrix). Define a functional

F_{\varepsilon}:Z\times H^{1}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{3})\times T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\mathbb{R}^{10}

by

(3.10)

F_{\varepsilon}(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta):=\begin{pmatrix}F^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta)\\
F^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}+w_{\varepsilon}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{10}\beta_{i}q_{i}\\
\left(\langle w_{\varepsilon},q_{1}\rangle,\cdots,\langle w_{\varepsilon},q_{10}\rangle\right)\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, the unknown
\displaystyle(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)
can be implicitly defined by the following equation

F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)=(0,0)^{\mathrm{T}}.

Let

G_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)=F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)-\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)[(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)],

we have

F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)[(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)]+G_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)=0.

Note that
\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta)=0
implies that for all
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z
,
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}+w_{\varepsilon}\right)\in T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z
and
\displaystyle w_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\perp T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z
.

Now, for a fixed
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z
, we consider the derivatives of
\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}
evaluated on
\displaystyle(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0)
.
For
\displaystyle\forall\ (v_{\varepsilon},\gamma)\in H^{1}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\mathbb{R}^{10}
, there holds

\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)[v_{\varepsilon},\gamma]=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial F^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)[v_{\varepsilon},\gamma]\\
\frac{\partial F^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)[v_{\varepsilon},\gamma]\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[v_{\varepsilon}]-\sum_{i=1}^{10}\gamma_{i}q_{i}\\
\left(\langle v_{\varepsilon},q_{1}\rangle,\cdots,\langle v_{\varepsilon},q_{10}\rangle\right)\end{pmatrix}.

We now show that
\displaystyle\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)
is a one-to-one and onto map.

Firstly, suppose that

\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)[v_{\varepsilon},\gamma]=(0,0)^{\mathrm{T}},

which implies

I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[v_{\varepsilon}]=\sum_{i=1}^{10}\gamma_{i}q_{i},\quad\langle v_{\varepsilon},q_{i}\rangle=0,~i=1,\dots,10,

thus
\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)
is orthogonal to
\displaystyle T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z
. From Lemma
3.2
, we deduce that
\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}=0
and consequently,

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}=0
. Hence,
\displaystyle\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)
is injective.

Secondly, we show that
\displaystyle\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)
is surjective. For given
\displaystyle(\tilde{w},\tilde{\gamma})\in H^{1}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\mathbb{R}^{10}
, we need to find
\displaystyle(v_{\varepsilon},\gamma)\in H^{1}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\mathbb{R}^{10}
such that

(3.11)

I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[v_{\varepsilon}]-\sum_{i=1}^{10}\gamma_{i}q_{i}=\tilde{w},\quad(\langle v_{\varepsilon},q_{1}\rangle,\cdots,\langle v_{\varepsilon},q_{10}\rangle)=\tilde{\gamma}.

Let

v_{\varepsilon}=v_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}+v_{\varepsilon}^{(2)},

where
\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\perp T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z
and
\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\in T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z
. Then,

\left(\langle v_{\varepsilon}^{(2)},q_{1}\rangle,\cdots,\langle v_{\varepsilon}^{(2)},q_{10}\rangle\right)=\tilde{\gamma}.

Choosing
\displaystyle\gamma_{i}=-\langle\tilde{w},q_{i}\rangle
, we obtain

\left(\tilde{w}+\sum_{i=1}^{10}\gamma_{i}q_{i}\right)\perp T_{z_{\mathcal{A}}}Z.

By Lemma
3.2
, we have

v_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}=\left(I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\right)^{-1}\left(\tilde{w}+\sum_{i=1}^{10}\gamma_{i}q_{i}\right),

therefore, (
3.11
) has a solution and
\displaystyle\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)
is onto. Therefore,
\displaystyle\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)
is uniformly reversible for
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon
sufficiently small. Consequently, we can write

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)\right)^{-1}[G_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)]

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)\right)^{-1}[F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)+H_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)]

\displaystyle:=W_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right),

where

H_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)=F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)-F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)-\frac{\partial F_{\varepsilon}}{\partial(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)[(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)].

Then
\displaystyle H_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)
satisfies

(3.12)

\begin{cases}\|H_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)\|\leq C\|(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)\|^{2},\\
\|H_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)-H_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},\bar{w}_{\varepsilon},\bar{\beta}\right)\|\leq C\left(\|(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)\|+\|(\bar{w}_{\varepsilon},\bar{\beta})\|\right)\|(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)-(\bar{w}_{\varepsilon},\bar{\beta})\|,\end{cases}

where
\displaystyle C=C(\mathcal{D},g,\bar{C})
is a constant.

Using (
3.12
), we can prove that the map
\displaystyle W_{\varepsilon}
is a contraction
in a ball of radius
\displaystyle\tilde{C}\|F_{\varepsilon}(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0)\|
for some positive constant
\displaystyle\tilde{C}
. Hence, there exists a fixed point
\displaystyle(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)
such that

(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)=W_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right).

That is,
\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)=0
. Since
\displaystyle\|F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)\|\leq C\|I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\|
for some constant
\displaystyle C
and
\displaystyle\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},w_{\varepsilon},\beta\right)\in B_{\tilde{C}\|F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)\|}
, we have

\|(w_{\varepsilon},\beta)\|\leq\tilde{C}\|F_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}},0,0\right)\|\leq C\|I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\|.

Thus, we conclude that

\|w_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\|\leq C\|I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\|.

∎

In order to control the norm of
\displaystyle w_{\varepsilon}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)
in Proposition
3.3
, we estimate
\displaystyle\left\|I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\right\|
for
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z
.

Lemma 3.4
.

For
\displaystyle\varepsilon
sufficiently small and for all
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z
, there holds

\|I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\|\leq e(\varepsilon),

where
\displaystyle e(\varepsilon):=O\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\left(\mu_{i}^{2}\mu_{j}^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+\sum_{l=1}^{2}\left(|a_{i,l}|\mu_{i}\mu_{j}^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}d^{-2}+|p_{i,l}|\mu_{i}^{4}\mu_{j}^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}d^{-5}\right)\right)\right)=O\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{4}}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle v\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{A}}\in Z
. The functional
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)
is given by

(3.13)

I_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)[v]=\int_{\mathcal{D}}\nabla z_{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\nabla v+2\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left(\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)_{y}\right)+2\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}}z_{\mathcal{A}}\cdot(v_{x}\wedge g_{y}+g_{x}\wedge v_{y})+2\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot(g_{x}\wedge g_{y}).

For the first two terms in (
3.13
), integrating by parts, we have

(3.14)

\int_{\mathcal{D}}\nabla z_{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\nabla v+2\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)_{y}\right]=\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[-\Delta z_{\mathcal{A}}+2\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)_{y}\right].

Since

\Delta z_{\mathcal{A}}=\Delta(P\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}})=\Delta\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}=2\left(\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}\right)_{y},

we have

(3.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[-\Delta z_{\mathcal{A}}+2\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(z_{\mathcal{A}}\right)_{y}\right]

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[\left(z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(z_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}\right)_{y}\right]+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[\left(\mathcal{Q}_{i}\varphi_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(\mathcal{Q}_{i}\varphi_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}\right)_{y}\right]

\displaystyle-2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[\left(\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(\mathcal{Q}_{i}\varphi_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}\right)_{y}+\left(\mathcal{Q}_{i}\varphi_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}\right)_{y}\right].

Moreover, for the terms containing the boundary function
\displaystyle g=\sum_{i=1}^{k}g^{(i)}(z,\omega^{(i)})
in (
3.13
), integrating by parts, one has

(3.16)

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}}z_{\mathcal{A}}\cdot(v_{x}\wedge g_{y}+g_{x}\wedge v_{y})+2\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot(g_{x}\wedge g_{y})

\displaystyle=2\varepsilon\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{D}}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)\cdot\left[v_{x}\wedge\left(g^{(j)}\right)_{y}+\left(g^{(j)}\right)_{x}\wedge v_{y}\right]+2\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[\left(g^{(i)}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(g^{(j)}\right)_{y}\right]

\displaystyle+2\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{\mathcal{D}}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)\cdot\left[v_{x}\wedge\left(g^{(i)}\right)_{y}+\left(g^{(i)}\right)_{x}\wedge v_{y}\right]+2\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[\left(g^{(i)}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(g^{(i)}\right)_{y}\right].

We first consider the following terms in (
3.15
) and (
3.16
),

(3.17)

\displaystyle 2\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[\left(z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(z_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}\right)_{y}\right]

\displaystyle+2\varepsilon\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{D}}\left(z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)\cdot\left[v_{x}\wedge\left(g^{(j)}\right)_{y}+\left(g^{(j)}\right)_{x}\wedge v_{y}\right]+2\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{D}}v\cdot\left[\left(g^{(i)}\right)_{x}\wedge\left(g^{(j)}\right)_{y}\right].

Recall that

z_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}=P\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)},a_{i},p_{i}}=P\mathcal{Q}_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i},\xi^{(i)}}+P\mathcal{Q}_{i}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}+P\mathcal{Q}_{i}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}},

where
